\subcaptionsetup

labelfont=bf,justification=centering



How Does the Pretraining Distribution Shape In-Context Learning?
Task Selection, Generalization, and Robustness



Waïss Azizian
††thanks: Work done during an internship at Morgan Stanley Machine Learning Research.
Affiliation: Univ. Grenoble Alpes, CNRS, Inria, Grenoble INP, LJK, 38000 Grenoble, France

  
Ali Hasan

Affiliation: waiss.azizian@univ-grenoble-alpes.fr  ali.hasan@morganstanley.com

Affiliation: Machine Learning Research, Morgan Stanley, New York, USA


Abstract
    

The emergence of in-context learning (ICL) in large language models (LLMs) remains poorly understood despite its consistent effectiveness, enabling models to adapt to new tasks from only a handful of examples. To clarify and improve these capabilities, we characterize how the statistical properties of the pretraining distribution (e.g., tail behavior, coverage) shape ICL on numerical tasks. We develop a theoretical framework that unifies task selection and generalization, extending and sharpening earlier results, and show how distributional properties govern sample efficiency, task retrieval, and robustness.
To this end, we generalize Bayesian posterior consistency and concentration results to heavy-tailed priors and dependent sequences, better reflecting the structure of LLM pretraining data. We then empirically study how ICL performance varies with the pretraining distribution on challenging tasks such as stochastic differential equations and stochastic processes with memory. Together, these findings suggest that controlling key statistical properties of the pretraining distribution is essential for building ICL-capable and reliable LLMs.

  




1 Introduction




In-context learning (ICL) is the phenomenon whereby a model generalizes to a new task from a handful of examples provided in the input context without any model weight updates. This emergent behavior has been observed across models in multiple domains, including in language (Brown et al., 2020), vision (Radford et al., 2021), and reinforcement learning (Moeini et al., 2025).
ICL is a particularly appealing feature in domains where data for a specific task is scarce such as robotics (Ahn et al., 2023b), healthcare (Singhal et al., 2023), or chemistry (Stokes et al., 2020).




Despite growing interest, the conditions under which ICL emerges are still poorly understood. Several lines of works have emerged to address this question.
The algorithmic view focuses on studying which learning algorithms over the context can be implemented by transformer and thereby perform ICL (Garg et al., 2022; Akyürek et al., 2023). Others have suggested modeling ICL as Bayesian inference (Xie et al., 2021; Lin and Lee, 2024; Zhang et al., 2025b; Jeon et al., 2024).
Empirical works have sought to design controlled settings in which ICL can be carefully studied, and these works highlight how sensitive to pretraining choices ICL is (Chan et al., 2022; Raventós et al., 2023), indicating that distributional aspects of pretraining play a central role. A crucial line of work also seeks to assess ICL performance on numerical tasks through out-of-distribution robustness of ICL (Wang et al., 2025b; Kwon et al., 2025; Goddard et al., 2025) but its behavior remains poorly understood.




Yet existing modeling frameworks often focus on restricted settings and lack general tools that links properties of the pretraining distribution to ICL behavior at test time. Three aspects remain particularly underexplored: (i) heavy-tailed distributions that better reflect real-world pretraining corpora and have been identified as key drivers of ICL (Chan et al., 2022; Singh et al., 2023), (ii) non-i.i.d. and dependent structures (e.g., long-range dependencies in language sequences) that fall outside standard i.i.d. or Markovian ICL modeling (Alabdulmohsin et al., 2024), and (iii) how these distributional properties govern the robustness of ICL under shifts at test time, which is a key feature of ICL (Wang et al., 2025b; Kwon et al., 2025; Goddard et al., 2025).




We thus develop a study of ICL with a focus on the influence of the pretraining distribution. We decompose ICL performance into two components: task selection (identifying the right task from the context) and generalization (performing well on tasks and sequences unseen during training) and focus on the following questions:




How does the pre-training distribution shape ICL performance on new tasks?
How does it affect task selection and generalization errors?




Our contributions are as follows:





• 


Framework. We develop a general theoretical framework for ICL that focuses on the role of pretraining distributional properties, handling both the task selection error and the ICL generalization error.




• 


Theory under heavy tails and dependence. We extend Bayesian consistency and concentration guarantees to heavy-tailed priors and dependent sequences, providing conditions that better reflect pretraining data used for LLMs and highlighting the role of these key distributional properties.




• 


Empirical validation on numerical tasks. We validate the framework on challenging numerical tasks—including stochastic differential equations and processes with memory, assessing ICL via robustness to new tasks and distribution shift, and finding outcomes consistent with our theory.







Together, our results suggest that controlling key statistical properties of the pretraining distribution is essential for building ICL-capable and reliable transformer models.







2 Related Work




A number of works study ICL through varying perspectives and definitions of ICL.
We will focus on the perspectives most relevant to what we study.




Conditions for ICL.



Other works devoted to studying the conditions under which ICL occurs.
From a pre-training perspective, Chan et al. (2022) studied the distribution qualities of a pretraining distribution that leads to ICL while Raventós et al. (2023) studied the influence of regularization and training distribution on linear regression tasks.
However, these do not consider a unified theory for predicting how ICL behaves under a particular pre-training distribution and only consider a limited class of experiments.
Singh et al. (2023) showed that ICL is transient and conditions must be carefully chosen such that the model performs ICL rather than in-weight learning.






Bayesian Perspectives.



From a statistical perspective, a series of questions were raised as to how ICL can be studied through a Bayesian framework where the pre-training distribution acts as a prior.
Xie et al. (2021) proposed viewing ICL as Bayesian model averaging.
Lin and Lee (2024) studied how ICL involves two modes of operation where one case the model generalizes and the other case the model retrieves similar tasks.
Zhang et al. (2025b) considered a theory for a Bayesian perspective of ICL and provided error bounds on the task loss as a function of the number of tasks and the number of points within each task.
However, they do not study specific properties of the pre-training distribution that lead to good ICL performance.
Jeon et al. (2024) provide an information theoretic perspective on task retrieval for ICL but do not model the distribution of tasks.






Generalization.



Several works have studied the generalization properties of ICL. Li et al. (2023) obtain such results by studying the stability of the transformer architecture but they consider the same fixed and finite task distribution during both pre-training and testing.
Zhang et al. (2025b); Zekri et al. (2024) both provide generalization bounds for ICL on Markov chains but without modelling the distribution of tasks during pre-training, which is our focus here.
Lotfi et al. (2024) provide generalization bounds for transformers on arbitrary sequences but with a restrictive notion of generalization that does not capture the ICL setting.






Numerical Tasks.



Related to the experiments we consider is a line of work studies ICL on small transformer models and simple tasks. Zhang et al. (2024); Wu et al. (2024) study ICL on linear regression tasks with a single-linear attention model, characterizing the ICL error of the trained model and the sample complexity of learning ICL.
Chan et al. (2025) study a simple model of a Bayesian predictor to understand the different modes of in-weight learning and ICL.






Algorithms and Out of Distribution.



Several works focus on the training dynamics of transformers for ICL, as well as how the transformer architecture is expressive enough to implement a wide variety of algorithms for ICL.
This is an important and desirable quality since it would allow for generalization across out of distribution tasks.
Wang et al. (2025b); Kwon et al. (2025); Goddard et al. (2025) all study this question from different perspectives and ultimately conclude that certain conditions on the pretraining distribution allow for some level of out of distribution performance.
We defer a more detailed review of these works to Appendix A.






General Concentration Results.



Finally, we briefly review relevant concentration results. The pioneering work of Yu (1994) provides concentration inequalities for dependent processes with a total variation condition, opening up a fruitful line of research, see e.g., Kontorovich and Ramanan (2008); Mohri and Rostamizadeh (2008); Mohri and Rostamizadeh (2010); Maurer (2023); Abélès et al. (2025) and, for related coupling techniques, see (Chazottes et al., 2007; Paulin, 2015), as well as references therein.
Though these frameworks can handle non-linear functions of dependent sequences, they require boundedness assumptions that are not suitable for our setting.
Another line of work has studied so-called functional dependence conditions (Wu, 2005; Wu, 2011) and provided concentration inequalities for sums of stationary dependent sequences (Liu et al., 2013). However, our ICL setting requires concentration inequalities for more general function classes and non-stationary sequences, which to the best of our knowledge are not available in the literature.
Concerning heavy-tailed concentration bounds, we refer to the recent frameworks of Bakhshizadeh et al. (2023); Li and Liu (2024b); Li et al. (2024); Li and Liu (2024b) which provide concentration inequalities for non-linear functions of independent heavy-tailed random variables and which we extend to the dependent setting.









3 Theoretical framework





3.1 In-context learning setting




In line with existing ICL works, we model the training data as a mixture of tasks, with each task defining its own distribution.
Formally, denote by {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Theta}\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}Θ⊂ℝd{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Theta}\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d} the space of tasks {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta} and by {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})π⁡(θ){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}) the density of the pretraining task distribution.
Given a task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}, the data is generated according to a task-specific distribution with density \density(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})p(⋅|θ)\density(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})
The training data is then generated by first sampling a task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta} from the task distribution {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}, and then sampling data points ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\geq 1}(xt)t≥1({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\geq 1} according to






{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}+1}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})\,,\quad\text{ where }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}).xt+1∼p#​1(⋅|x1:t,θ), where x1:t=(x1,…,xt).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}+1}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})\,,\quad\text{ where }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}).






We first present some running examples to illustrate the setting.



Example 3.1 (Classification).


Several ICL benchmarks for LLMs such as Bertsch et al. (2025); Zou et al. (2025); Li et al. (2025b) are built on classification tasks.
Each task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta} represents a small subset of classes from a larger classification problem and the data sequence {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}x1,…,xt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} is a sequence of inputs and labels from these classes.
The challenge is therefore to both identify the classes and learn to classify them from the in-context examples.




Example 3.2 (Linear Regression).


Introduced by Garg et al. (2022), the regression setting is a popular testbed for ICL.
Each task \theta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}θ∈ℝd\theta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d} defines a linear model {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}+\epsilony=θT​q+ϵ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}+\epsilon where \epsilonϵ\epsilon is some noise.
The data sequence {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}x1,…,x2​t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}} is a sequence of input-output pairs {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}q1,y1,…,qt,yt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} generated according to the linear model defined by {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}.




Example 3.3 (Next-sample prediction for stochastic processes).


More generally, we can consider the setting where each task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta} defines a stochastic process {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}+1}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})xt+1∼p#​1(⋅|x1:t,θ){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}+1}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}). We will consider later the specific case of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process: each task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}=(\tau,\mu)θ=(τ,μ){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}=(\tau,\mu) defines a mean-reverting stochastic process with mean \muμ\mu and reversion speed \tauτ\tau:






\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\tau(\mu-X_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}W_{t}\,,d​Xt=τ⁡(μ−Xt)​d​t+σ​d​Wt,\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\tau(\mu-X_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}W_{t}\,,

(1)



where W_{t}WtW_{t} is a standard Brownian motion and \sigmaσ\sigma is the volatility parameter. The data sequence {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}x1,…,xt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} is then a discretization of the stochastic process defined by {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}.
In this setting, the learning objective is to both identify the parameters of the stochastic process and predict the next sample given the previous ones.
We will also consider more intricate processes that are not Markovian.





Given a dataset of tasks {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}θ1,…,θN{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N} and associated samples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{(1)},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N})}x1:T(1),…,x1:T(N){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{(1)},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N})}, a model {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}f{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}} is trained by minimizing the next-sample prediction loss






{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}},({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})=\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})\,,L^(f,(θn,x1:Tn)n≤N)=1N​T∑n=1N∑t=1Tℓt(f(x1:t−1n),xtn),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}},({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})=\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})\,,

(2)



where {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}ℓt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} is a per-sample loss which depend on ttt to encompass regression and classification tasks. Note that the model is trained to predict the next sample {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}xt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} given the previous samples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}x1:t−1{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}, without any explicit supervision on the task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}. This is why ICL is referred to as an emergent ability of large models (Wei et al., 2022).




We consider two kinds of error for ICL: (i) the ability of the model to identify the correct task given some in-context examples, which we refer to as task selection, and (ii) the generalization error of the trained model {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}}f^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}} obtained by minimizing Eq. 2 on a training dataset, which we refer to as generalization error.
We first study task selection, before turning to the generalization error, which is more involved.







3.2 Task selection




Our first main result concerns the ability of a trained model to perform ICL and in particular to retrieve the correct task given some input sequence.
For this, we adopt the Bayesian point of view, similarly to Lin and Lee (2024); Zekri et al. (2024); Jeon et al. (2024); Zhang et al. (2025b); Wang et al. (2025b). Indeed, if {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}f{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}} is arbitrarily powerful and trained to optimality, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}f{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}} learns the Bayesian optimal predictor. If we denote the posterior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{p}}_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1})p^#​1(θ|x1:t−1){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{p}}_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}) the posterior distribution over tasks given the input sequence {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}x1:t−1{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}, the Bayesian optimal predictor is given by








\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1})=f(x1:t−1)=\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1})=
\displaystyle\argmin_{\hat{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}\ex_{#1}\left[\ex_{#1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}(\hat{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})\right]\right]\,.arg​minx^t⁡𝔼#​1​[𝔼#​1⁡[ℓt​(x^t,xt)]].\displaystyle\argmin_{\hat{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}\ex_{#1}\left[\ex_{#1}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}(\hat{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})\right]\right]\,.

(3)





For a model to perform ICL given in-context examples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}x1:t−1{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1} generated from a task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}, it is therefore necessary that the posterior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{p}}_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1})p^#​1(θ|x1:t−1){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{p}}_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}) concentrates around the true task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*} as the number of in-context examples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} increases.
Our first main result provides a quantitative guarantee of this concentration and highlights the role of the properties of the pretraining distribution {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}.




For this, we require some mild assumptions on the data generation process only; they do not restrict the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}.
Since our focus is on the influence of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} on task identification, in the main text we mainly focus on assumptions and quantities that involve {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}, and defer the detailed assumptions to Appendix D.
We will therefore use the notation \poly(x)poly⁡(x)\poly(x) to denote a quantity that is polynomial in xxx with coefficients independent of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} and the number of samples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}.



Assumption 1 (Data generation, informal).


Let {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Theta}θ∗∈Θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Theta} be the true task. We assume:



(i) 


Tail control. Sequences {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}x1:t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}} generated under the true task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*} have controlled tails, at most \poly({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T})poly⁡(T)\poly({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}) on typical tail events and {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} admits a second moment.




(ii)

for any {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}>0R>0{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}>0, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}t≤T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T},

These assumptions are quite mild and are satisfied by our examples, see Section F.2.




As a metric to assess the quality of a given retrieved task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta} w.r.t. the true task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}, we consider the Rényi divergence (Rényi, 1961) of order {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}\in(0,1)ρ∈(0,1){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}\in(0,1) between the distributions \density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})p#​1(⋅|θ)\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}) and \density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})p#​1(⋅|θ∗)\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}):






\textstyle\mathrm{D}_{#1}\!({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\,\|\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})=-\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho})}\log\ex_{#1}\left[\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}=1}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\left(\tfrac{\density_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})}{\density_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})}\right)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}\right]\,.D#​1(θ∥θ∗)=−1T⁡(1−ρ)log𝔼#​1[∏t=1T(p#​1(xt|x1:t−1,θ)p#​1(xt|x1:t−1,θ∗))ρ].\textstyle\mathrm{D}_{#1}\!({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\,\|\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})=-\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho})}\log\ex_{#1}\left[\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}=1}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\left(\tfrac{\density_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta})}{\density_{#1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})}\right)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}\right]\,.




We divide by {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} to obtain a per-sample divergence that does not trivially diverge as {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} increases.




Our main theorem below shows that, under Assumption 1, the posterior distribution over tasks concentrates around the true task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*} as the number of in-context examples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} increases, at a rate that depends on the properties of the pretraining distribution {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}.



Theorem 1 (Task selection).


Let {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}\in(0,1)ρ∈(0,1){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}\in(0,1), under Assumption 1, with {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{\ast})>0π⁡(θ∗)>0{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{\ast})>0 and {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})x1:T∼p#​1(⋅|θ∗){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}), the posterior distribution over tasks satisfies






\displaystyle\ex_{#1}\left[\ex_{#1}\left[\mathrm{D}_{#1}\!({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\,\|\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})\right]\right]\leq𝔼#​1[𝔼#​1[D#​1(θ∥θ∗)]]≤\displaystyle\ex_{#1}\left[\ex_{#1}\left[\mathrm{D}_{#1}\!({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\,\|\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})\right]\right]\leq
\displaystyle\tfrac{1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{\log 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})}+\bigoh\left(\tfrac{\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\right)\,,1+ρ(1−ρ)​T​log⁡1/π⁡(θ∗)+𝒪⁡(log⁡TT),\displaystyle\tfrac{1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}}{(1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{\log 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})}+\bigoh\left(\tfrac{\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\right)\,,

(4)



where the terms in \bigoh\left(\frac{\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\right)𝒪⁡(log⁡TT)\bigoh\left(\frac{\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\right) do not depend on the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} or are negligible compared to the first term.





To place this result into context, Theorem 1 provides a guarantee on how close the posterior distribution over tasks is to the true task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*} as the number of in-context examples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} increases.
The  RHS (RHS) decays as \bigoh(1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T})𝒪⁡(1/T)\bigoh(1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}), which shows that the posterior concentrates around the true task as the number of examples in-context increases.
The speed of convergence is governed by the coefficient \log 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})log⁡1/π⁡(θ∗)\log 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}), which quantifies how well the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} covers the true task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}: the smaller {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})π⁡(θ∗){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}), the slower the convergence.
Since in ICL we wish to study the capabilities of learning a new task from in-context examples, this result quantifies the speed at which ICL learns this new task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}: the further {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*} is from the bulk of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}, the slower ICL learns this new task.
Thus, when learning with ICL, the ability to learn a new task and its robustness to new tasks therefore crucially depends on the tail of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}.
This simple statement thus captures a key aspect of ICL that was observed empirically in several works (Chan et al., 2022; Singh et al., 2023).




From a technical viewpoint, Theorem 1 is proven in Appendix D using ideas from Bayesian statistics (Zhang, 2003; Zhang, 2006) is extremely general, covers discrete and continuous task spaces, and does not require any probabilistic structure on the data sequence {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}x1:t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}} nor specific data distributions.
Moreover, unlike most existing results, Theorem 1 provides a guarantee on the posterior distribution given all {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} in-context examples, and not only on the regret, which bounds the average error of the posterior distributions given 1,\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}1,…,T1,\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} examples. This better reflects the practical use of ICL, where the user typically only considers the output of the model after all in-context examples have been provided.




Finally, we provide in the appendix, in Section D.4 a more refined version of Theorem 1 that involves not just the prior density at the true task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})π⁡(θ∗){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}) but also the local geometry of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} around {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}θ∗{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*}, which can provide much sharper bounds in some cases.
This refined result also encompasses the case where {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})=0π⁡(θ∗)=0{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{*})=0, in which the ICL error is not vanishing anymore.
In this scenario, it shows that ICL can struggle on out-of-distribution tasks, as empirically studied previously (Goddard et al., 2025; Kwon et al., 2025; Yadlowsky et al., 2023).




Takeaway #1: Heavier-tailed priors are beneficial for task identification and its robustness, as they improve the learning speed on new tasks.




We will now examine the generalization error of ICL and see that there is a trade-off.







3.3 Generalization error




The second key statistical question for ICL is its generalization error. For the trained transformer to accurately behave as the Bayesian optimal predictor w.r.t. the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}, it is necessary that the next-token prediction be minimized on the true data distribution, and not just on the training data.




We therefore study the generalization error of the trained model {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}}f^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}} obtained by minimizing Eq. 2 on a training dataset.
We consider a dataset consisting of {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}N{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N} tasks {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}θ1,…,θN{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N} sampled independently from the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}, and for each task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}θn{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}, a sequence of {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} samples {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}}x1:Tn{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}} generated according to the task-specific distribution \density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})p#​1(⋅|θn)\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}): for {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}n≤N{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}, for {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}<{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}t<T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}<{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}+1}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})xt+1(n)∼p#​1(⋅|x1:t(n),θn){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}+1}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})}\sim\density_{#1}(\cdot\nonscript\,|\nonscript\,\mathopen{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}^{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n})},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}).




To the best of our knowledge, existing concentration for dependent sequences do not cover this case.
We thus develop our own framework: we encompass non- iid (iid) and non-Markovian data sequences through a weak dependence assumption in Wasserstein distance, and we handle heavy-tailed task distributions by taking inspiration from the recent framework of Li and Liu (2024a); Li et al. (2024). The resulting framework is therefore quite general and can be of independent interest beyond ICL, see Appendix E.




Here we again present a simplified version of our assumptions, where we focus on the few key quantities that are relevant in our study: how dependent the data sequence is and how heavy-tailed the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} is, quantified through the maximal moment of {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} that exists**
              *
              
              
              
              
              
              
              
            We focus here on prior distributions with polynomially decaying tails, such as the Student-ttt family, since it is the most representative. A similar result could be established for priors with subexponential tails..
We refer to Section E.3 for the complete version of the assumptions.
We consider {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}}ℱ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}} a class of models {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}:\cup_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k})^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}f:∪t(ℝk)t→ℝk{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}:\cup_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k})^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k} and {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}_{+}ℓt:ℝk×ℝk→ℝ+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}_{+} a per-sample loss function that can depend on time {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}.

(v) 


Usual conditions. The losses {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}ℓt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} are 1-Lipschitz; the class of models {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}}ℱ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}} is bounded and uniformly Lipschitz with respect to some metric and {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}xt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}} conditioned on {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}x1:t−1,θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta} is uniformly sub-Gaussian.








{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}AT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}BT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}, and {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}LT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} are the key quantities that govern the generalization error of ICL.
When {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} has polynomial tails, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q} quantifies how heavy-tailed the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} is: the smaller {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}, the heavier the tail of {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}. For Student’s ttt-distribution with \nuν\nu degrees of freedom, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}=\lfloor\nu-1\rfloorq=⌊ν−1⌋{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}=\lfloor\nu-1\rfloor.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}BT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} quantifies how dependent the data sequence is while {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}AT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} also quantifies how much the task influences the data distribution: in the case of an iid sequence,
both {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}AT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} and {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}BT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} are bounded w.r.t. {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}, which might not be the case in general.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}LT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} quantifies how much the model {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}f{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}} uses the older examples in context: for transformer with context length at least {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}LT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} is typically bounded. If, on the contrary, the context length is kept constant and smaller than {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}, as in Zekri et al. (2024), {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}LT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} can decay as 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}1/T1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}.
In particular, Assumption 2 skips the assumptions on the size of the hypothesis class {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}}ℱ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}} since this is not our main focus, and we refer to the appendix for details.




Our main result provides a bound on the generalization error of the trained model {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}}f^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}}:






\widehat{\mathrm{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{gen}}}}\coloneqq\ex_{#1}\bigg[\ex_{#1}\bigg[\tfrac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}=1}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})\bigg]\bigg]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}},({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})\,,gen^≔𝔼#​1[𝔼#​1[1T∑t=1Tℓt(f^(x1:t−1),xt)]]−L^(f^,(θn,x1:Tn)n≤N),\widehat{\mathrm{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{gen}}}}\coloneqq\ex_{#1}\bigg[\ex_{#1}\bigg[\tfrac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}=1}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}-1}),{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}})\bigg]\bigg]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}},({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}})\,,

(8)



for {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}}f^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}}} being the model obtained using the empirical distribution ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}(θn,x1:Tn)n≤N({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{1:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}})_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}.



Theorem 2.


Under Assumption 2, for any {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\in(0,e^{-2})δ∈(0,e−2){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\in(0,e^{-2}), with probability at least 1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}1−δ1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}, it holds:



(a) 


If {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}e^{-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}δ≥N​e−q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}e^{-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}, then






\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{gen}}}\leq\bigoh\left(\frac{(\log 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})^{3/2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}}{\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\left(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\right)\right)\,,gen^≤𝒪⁡((log⁡1/δ)3/2​LT​TN​(1+AT​T+BT​T)),\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{gen}}}\leq\bigoh\left(\frac{(\log 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})^{3/2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}}{\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\left(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\right)\right)\,,

(9)





(b) 


If {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}<{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}e^{-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}δ<N​e−q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}<{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}e^{-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}, then






\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{gen}}}\leq\bigoh\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\left(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\right)\right)\,,gen^≤𝒪⁡(LT​Tδ1/q​N​(1+AT​T+BT​T)),\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{gen}}}\leq\bigoh\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}}}\left(1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\right)\right)\,,

(10)



where the terms in \bigoh(\cdot)𝒪⁡(⋅)\bigoh(\cdot) depend polynomially on {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}, \log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}log⁡N\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}, the scale of {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} and the size of {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}}ℱ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}}.








Like standard concentration inequalities for sums of independent heavy-tailed random variables,
Theorem 2 provides two regimes. For small deviations, i.e., {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}δ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta} not arbitrarily small, the generalization error behaves like in a sub-exponential setting. However, for large deviations, i.e., {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}δ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta} very small, the behaviour of the generalization error worsens and depends on the moment {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q} of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}.




The generalization thus depends critically on the moment {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q} of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}:
the smaller the moment {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}q{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}, the heavier the tail of the prior {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} and the worse the generalization error. This provides a counterpoint to the task selection result of Theorem 1 that showed that heavier-tailed priors are beneficial for task identification. This highlights a fundamental trade-off in the choice of the pretraining distribution {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}: heavier-tailed priors are beneficial for task identification, but harm the generalization error.




This bound also highlights how much larger the number of tasks must be compared to the number of in-context examples to ensure good generalization: in general, one needs {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}N{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N} to be at least much larger than {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} to ensure a small generalization error. This is in line with our experiments and previous empirical studies Raventós et al. (2023): to obtain optimal ICL performance with a context length of 16 or 64, one needs thousands of tasks.
Moreover, if the data sequence is highly dependent, i.e., {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}AT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}A}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} and {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}BT{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}B}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T} are large, the requirement on the number of tasks {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}N{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N} for ICL to generalize well also increases. This will be demonstrated in Section 4.3.




Takeaway #2: Heavier-tailed priors and stronger temporal dependences increase the number of tasks required for reliable ICL generalization.










4 Experiments




We conduct a series of experiments to empirically study the behavior of the pretraining distribution on the performance of ICL††
            †
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          Additional results and figures are in Appendix B..
We aim to answer two main questions: do the qualitative characteristics of the proposed bounds hold in practice? and; how do modifications of the pretraining distribution affect performance as the test distribution changes in distance from the pretraining distribution?
To do this, we train a transformer under different pretraining distributions to solve different ICL tasks.



Figure 1: Influence of the degree of freedom parameter of a Student-ttt pretraining distribution on the ICL error for different task shifts with and without importance weighting. Weighted samples given by -\star−⁣⋆-\star marker.





(a) n=500n=500n=500


(b) n=1000n=1000n=1000


(c) n=5000n=5000n=5000

Figure 2: Generalization for linear regression with a Student-ttt prior of varying \nuν\nu as a function of nnn.



ICL evaluation through robustness to distribution shift.



The transformer is trained on tasks \thetaθ\theta sampled from a pretraining distribution {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}.
To assess the ICL performance, we evaluate the trained model on tasks \theta^{\prime}=\theta+\Deltaθ′=θ+Δ\theta^{\prime}=\theta+\Delta where {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi}θ∼π{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pi} and \DeltaΔ\Delta is a deterministic shift and report the ICL error on these shifted tasks as a function of the shift magnitude \|\Delta\|‖Δ‖\|\Delta\|
Studying this error as a function of the shape of the pretraining distribution allows us to validate the theory in Theorem 1.
We also study the performance of ICL as a function of the number of pretraining tasks to test how well the methods generalize, with an emphasis on relating the theory in Theorem 2.






Distributions and Metrics.



The pretraining distributions and their parameter values are given in Table 1.
The parameters are chosen such that changing them produces a change in the shape of the pretraining distribution.
In both cases, lower parameter values indicate heavier tails of the distribution.
The scale parameter is chosen such that all pretraining distributions have the same variance.
For all experiments, we consider mean squared error (MSE) as the metric we compare.
We also consider the best MSE over the context length, which is given by \min_{t}(\hat{f}(x_{t})-x_{t+1})^{2}mint⁡(f^​(xt)−xt+1)2\min_{t}(\hat{f}(x_{t})-x_{t+1})^{2}; the mean MSE given by \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\hat{f}(x_{t})-x_{t+1})^{2}1T​∑t=1T(f^​(xt)−xt+1)2\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\hat{f}(x_{t})-x_{t+1})^{2}; and finally the full context length MSE given by (\hat{f}(x_{T-1})-x_{T})^{2}(f^​(xT−1)−xT)2(\hat{f}(x_{T-1})-x_{T})^{2}.
These allow us to see how the different priors perform while taking into consideration the full context length.

4.1 Linear Regression




We first consider the linear regression setting introduced in Example 3.2 where each {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d}θ∈ℝd{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d} defines a linear regression task {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{i}+\epsilon_{i}yi=θT​qi+ϵi{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q}_{i}+\epsilon_{i} for i=1,...,64i=1,…,64i=1,...,64 where 64 is the context length.
During pretraining, we sample {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}θ{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta} according to four different distributions, where the distributions have the same location and scale but different tail decay.
We consider Student-ttt distributions with different shape parameters.
In Fig. 1, we see that the performance for small task shifts, the normal distribution prior is the highest performing, but for larger shifts the heavier tailed distributions perform better.



Figure 3: Influence of the degree of freedom parameter of a Student-ttt pretraining distribution on the ICL error for different task shifts with and without importance weighting for predicting the next step in an OU process with context length of 32. Weighted samples indicated by the -\star−⁣⋆-\star marker.


Figure 4: Influence of the shape of a generalized normal pretraining distribution on the ICL error for different task shifts with and without importance weighting for predicting the next step in an OU process.



Reweighting.



To further investigate the predictions of Theorem 1, we consider reweighting the pretraining distribution:
if we are given samples from a distribution PPP
but know that a pretraining distribution QQQ exhibits strong performance, can we improve the performance of distribution PPP by matching QQQ via importance sampling i.e. \mathbb{E}_{Q}[\ell(X)]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\ell(Y)\frac{\mathrm{d}Q}{\mathrm{d}P}\right]𝔼Q​[ℓ⁡(X)]=𝔼P​[ℓ⁡(Y)​d​Qd​P]\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\ell(X)]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\ell(Y)\frac{\mathrm{d}Q}{\mathrm{d}P}\right]?
We study this in Fig. 1 where we reweigh samples such that they are approximately uniform over the support of the empirical distribution.
The results indicate small improvement in the performance under large shifts using the reweighting as compared to without reweighting.






Generalization.



We next consider how the error behaves as the number of pretraining tasks changes.
This allows us to see under which regime the tail parameter plays an important role, as Theorem 2 suggests heavier-tailed pretraining distributions require more samples to generalize.
We illustrate this in Fig. 2 for n\in\{500,1000,5000\}n∈{500,1000,5000}n\in\{500,1000,5000\}.
The results validate the theory and indicate that for a smaller number of tasks, the lighter tail prior performs better whereas with more tasks the performance of the heavier tailed prior is improved.









4.2 Linear Stochastic Differential Equations




In the next set of experiments, we follow the setup in Example 3.3 with a stochastic process satisfying (1).
For our metric of success, we compare (\hat{X}_{t+1}-\mathbb{E}[X_{t+1}\mid X_{t}])^{2}(X^t+1−𝔼⁡[Xt+1∣Xt])2(\hat{X}_{t+1}-\mathbb{E}[X_{t+1}\mid X_{t}])^{2} where \hat{X}_{t+1}X^t+1\hat{X}_{t+1} is conditioned on the context of X_{s<t}Xs<tX_{s<t}.
We consider \theta,\muθ,μ\theta,\mu sampled from different pretraining distributions and again compare the performance of ICL on different test tasks.
We study both the Student-ttt distribution in Fig. 3 and the generalized normal in Fig. 4.
In both instances, we see that the heavier tailed pretraining distribution performs better for larger distribution shifts.
In the generalized normal case, the effect of reweighting is practically negligible, but in the Student-ttt case, we see some benefit, particularly in the large shift regime.







4.3 Stochastic Volterra Equations




We finally consider stochastic Volterra equations as a model of nonlinear stochastic processes that have long range dependencies.
These processes are, under certain conditions, known to model fractional Brownian motion, which exhibit self-similarity which has been thought to represent the distribution of tokens in LLMs (Alabdulmohsin et al., 2024).
Each task \thetaθ\theta parametrizes a multi-layer perceptron b_{\theta}bθb_{\theta}
and induces the process: X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\alpha}b_{\theta}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\alpha}\mathrm{d}W_{s},Xt=X0+∫0t(t−s)−α​bθ​(Xs)​𝑑s+∫0t(t−s)−α​d​Ws,X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\alpha}b_{\theta}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{-\alpha}\mathrm{d}W_{s},
where W_{t}WtW_{t} is a standard Brownian motion and \alpha>0α>0\alpha>0 controls the temporal dependence of the process: the smaller \alphaα\alpha is, the more past values influence the current value.
The dependency coefficients in Theorem 2 thus depend explicitly on \alphaα\alpha, they are larger for smaller \alphaα\alpha, see Section F.1.
We consider the generalization capabilities as a function of the number of pretraining tasks in Fig. 5 and as a function of \alphaα\alpha.
Theorem 2 predicts that generalization should suffer for smaller \alphaα\alpha due to the increased dependencies, which is validated in the experiments: the performance gap between the different \alphaα\alpha is larger for smaller number of tasks.






(a) n=500n=500n=500


(b) n=1000n=1000n=1000


(c) n=5000n=5000n=5000

Figure 5: Generalization of a transformer trained to predict the next step of the Volterra as a function of nnn the number of tasks with context length of 32.








5 Conclusion




In this work we study ICL through the perspective of task selection and generalization.
Our main theoretical contributions describe error bounds of ICL in terms of both task selection and generalization.
We show that a pre-training distribution must be carefully chosen such that the effects of both of these error terms are appropriately balanced.
Consequently, the theory allows one to explicitly design a prior distribution based on robustness considerations.
We design experiments which consider to what extent ICL can generalize on new tasks that may be out of distribution.
The key takeaways are that a heavier tailed prior is appropriate when considering distribution shifts or when many task examples are available.
These experiments shed light on how to appropriately pre-train transformers for their use with ICL, with specific emphasis on numerical tasks.




Limitations and Future Directions
While our theoretical results are general, the experiments are limited to numerical data: it remains to be seen how this applies to training LLMs when large numbers of documents need to be considered.
The reweighting experiments most closely correspond to the possible interventions one may make during pre-training or fine-tuning to improve ICL.
A natural follow-up study would consider how to leverage these insights to improve ICL on LLMs with tokens rather than continuous numerical data.

Appendix A Additional Related Work




Training dynamics of ICL



Varre et al. (2025) shows that nnn-grams are approximate stationary points in the training of two-layers transformers.
Zhang et al. (2025a) studies the training dynamics of a one-layer linear transformer with linear attention on linear regression tasks.
Sander et al. (2024) characterize the training dynamics of a one-linear layer transformer on auto-regressive tasks, showing how ICL emerges.
Ahn et al. (2023a) show that for linear regression problems and a linear transformer, the global minimizer of the training loss corresponds to performing one step of preconditioned gradient descent.
In contrast, our approach focuses on the influence of the pre-training distribution on ICL. We therefore assume that the model is sufficiently expressive and trained optimally enough to approximate the Bayes optimal predictor. We refer to recent works on optimization dynamics of transformers Gao et al. (2024); Barboni et al. (2025); Azizian et al. (2025) and on the approximation capabilities of transformers.






Approximation capabilities of transformers



The foundational works of Von Oswald et al. (2023); Akyürek et al. (2023) demonstrate that transformers can implement gradient descent. This has led to a fruitful line of work studying the algorithmic capabilities of transformers.
Bai et al. (2023) show that transformers can implement a wide variety of statistical methods.
Wang et al. (2025a) shows how transformers can implement functional gradient descent on categorical data, generalizing previous works.
Wu et al. (2025) shows how attention transformers can implement gradient descent on a ReLU network.
Sander and Peyré (2025) explicitly constructs a transformer that implements kernel causal regression.
On a more abstract perspective,
Furuya et al. (2025); Kratsios and Furuya (2025) show that (causal) transformers can approximate any (causal) map between measures.
Wang and Weinan (2024) studies quantitatively the approximation properties of transformers on "sparse memory" target functions.
Li et al. (2025a) obtains explicit approximation bounds for numerical ICL tasks.










Appendix B Additional Experimental Results





B.1 Linear Regression




We provide comprehensive experimental results for linear regression tasks (detailed in Section 4.1) using Student-ttt and generalized normal pretraining distributions.
This section presents the ICL error as a function of context length (ICL step) for Student-ttt priors with degrees of freedom \nu\in\{3,5,10,\infty\}ν∈{3,5,10,∞}\nu\in\{3,5,10,\infty\} and generalized normal priors with shape parameters \beta\in\{1,1.5,2,2.5\}β∈{1,1.5,2,2.5}\beta\in\{1,1.5,2,2.5\}, corresponding to the experimental settings in Fig. 1.




The results in Fig. 6 clearly demonstrate the fundamental trade-off in selecting pretraining distributions for ICL: heavy-tailed priors (small \nuν\nu) achieve superior performance under distribution shift, while light-tailed priors (large \nuν\nu) excel on in-distribution tasks.
In contrast, Fig. 7 shows that varying the shape parameter of generalized normal priors produces more subtle effects on ICL performance in the linear regression setting.




We also notice on Figs. 6 and 7 that longer context lengths are mostly beneficial for in-distribution tasks: as the perturbation magnitude increases, the performance gains from longer contexts diminish. This is in line with Section 3.2: the performance gain per new example is determined by the prior probability of the task, which decreases with larger perturbations.






(a) Student-t, \nu=3.0ν=3.0\nu=3.0


(b) Student-t, \nu=5.0ν=5.0\nu=5.0


(c) Student-t, \nu=10.0ν=10.0\nu=10.0


(d) Gaussian limit, \nu=\inftyν=∞\nu=\infty

Figure 6: Linear regression with Student-ttt pretraining distributions: MSE as a function of ICL step for different task shift magnitudes. Heavy-tailed priors (\nu=3ν=3\nu=3) show superior robustness to distribution shift, while light-tailed priors (\nu=\inftyν=∞\nu=\infty, Gaussian) perform better on unperturbed tasks. The Ridge regression baseline provides a reference that remains constant across perturbation magnitudes.





(a) GenNormal, \beta=1.0β=1.0\beta=1.0


(b) GenNormal, \beta=1.5β=1.5\beta=1.5


(c) GenNormal, \beta=2.0β=2.0\beta=2.0


(d) GenNormal, \beta=2.5β=2.5\beta=2.5

Figure 7: Linear regression with generalized normal pretraining distributions: MSE as a function of ICL step for different task shift magnitudes. The shape parameter \betaβ\beta has a more modest impact on performance compared to Student-ttt distributions, with all variants showing similar convergence patterns across perturbation levels.



We present an extended analysis of the generalization results from Fig. 2 in Fig. 8, examining how the number of pretraining tasks nnn affects performance across different Student-ttt tail parameters \nuν\nu. These results validate Theorem 2, showing that heavy-tailed priors require more training tasks to achieve comparable performance to light-tailed priors.






(a) n=100n=100n=100


(b) n=500n=500n=500


(c) n=1000n=1000n=1000


(d) n=5000n=5000n=5000

Figure 8: Generalization analysis for linear regression across different numbers of pretraining tasks nnn for a context length of 64. As predicted by Theorem 2, heavy-tailed priors (small \nuν\nu) require more tasks to achieve performance comparable to light-tailed priors, but eventually outperform them under distribution shift. The crossover point shifts to larger nnn for heavier-tailed distributions.



Finally, we provide an ablation study on the effect of the variance. All other experiments are designed so that the pretraining distribution has unit variance in each dimension. In Fig. 9, we vary the variance of a standard Gaussian pretraining distribution and observe it only changes the ICL performance for in-distribution tasks.



Figure 9: Ablation on the effect of variance for Gaussian pretraining distributions in linear regression. Only in-distribution performance is affected by the variance, with larger variances leading to worse performance.







B.2 Ornstein–Uhlenbeck Processes




We present detailed experimental results for Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) stochastic processes (described in Section 4.2) using both Student-ttt and generalized normal pretraining distributions.
The figures show ICL error as a function of context length for Student-ttt priors with degrees of freedom \nu\in\{3,5,10,\infty\}ν∈{3,5,10,∞}\nu\in\{3,5,10,\infty\} (matching Fig. 3) and generalized normal priors with shape parameters \beta\in\{1,1.5,2,2.5\}β∈{1,1.5,2,2.5}\beta\in\{1,1.5,2,2.5\} (matching Fig. 4) in Figs. 10 and 11, respectively.




Notably, OU processes exhibit different behavior compared to linear regression: the trade-off between in-distribution and out-of-distribution performance is less pronounced. As shown in both Figs. 10 and 11, heavy-tailed priors maintain competitive in-distribution performance while still providing improved robustness to distribution shift.






(a) Student-t, \nu=3.0ν=3.0\nu=3.0


(b) Student-t, \nu=5.0ν=5.0\nu=5.0


(c) Student-t, \nu=10.0ν=10.0\nu=10.0


(d) Gaussian limit, \nu=\inftyν=∞\nu=\infty

Figure 10: Ornstein–Uhlenbeck processes with Student-ttt pretraining distributions: MSE as a function of ICL step for different task shift magnitudes. Unlike linear regression, heavy-tailed priors maintain strong in-distribution performance while providing superior robustness to perturbations. Baselines include predicting the last observed value and fitting an ARMA(5) model to the context.





(a) GenNormal, \beta=1.0β=1.0\beta=1.0


(b) GenNormal, \beta=1.5β=1.5\beta=1.5


(c) GenNormal, \beta=2.0β=2.0\beta=2.0


(d) GenNormal, \beta=2.5β=2.5\beta=2.5

Figure 11: Ornstein–Uhlenbeck processes with generalized normal pretraining distributions (importance weighted): MSE as a function of ICL step for different task shift magnitudes. The shape parameter \betaβ\beta shows consistent effects across perturbation levels, with all variants significantly outperforming simple baselines. Importance weighting provides modest improvements in robustness.






B.3 Volterra Processes




We present comprehensive results for stochastic Volterra equations (detailed in Section 4.3), which model nonlinear processes with long-range dependencies and connections to fractional Brownian motion.
Figure 12 shows ICL error as a function of context length for different kernel exponents \alpha\in\{1,1.5,2\}α∈{1,1.5,2}\alpha\in\{1,1.5,2\}, where smaller \alphaα\alpha values correspond to stronger temporal dependencies.




The results confirm our theoretical predictions from Section 3: as the kernel exponent \alphaα\alpha increases (weaker dependencies), both convergence speed and final performance improve significantly. This validates the dependency structure analysis in Theorem 2.




Figure 13 extends the generalization analysis from Fig. 5, demonstrating how the number of pretraining tasks nnn interacts with the temporal dependency parameter \alphaα\alpha. The results show that processes with stronger dependencies (smaller \alphaα\alpha) require substantially more training data to achieve comparable performance.






(a) Kernel exponent \alpha=1.0α=1.0\alpha=1.0


(b) Kernel exponent \alpha=1.5α=1.5\alpha=1.5


(c) Kernel exponent \alpha=2.0α=2.0\alpha=2.0

Figure 12: Stochastic Volterra equations: MSE as a function of ICL step across different kernel exponents \alphaα\alpha. Smaller \alphaα\alpha values correspond to stronger long-range dependencies, leading to slower convergence and higher final error. The performance gap between different \alphaα\alpha values demonstrates the impact of temporal dependency structure on ICL learning. Simple baselines provide reference points for comparison.





(a) n=100n=100n=100


(b) n=500n=500n=500


(c) n=1000n=1000n=1000


(d) n=5000n=5000n=5000

Figure 13: Generalization analysis for Volterra processes across different numbers of pretraining tasks nnn. Processes with stronger temporal dependencies (smaller \alphaα\alpha) exhibit larger performance gaps at low nnn, consistent with Theorem 2. The dependency coefficients in our theory scale with \alphaα\alpha, explaining why more training tasks are needed to achieve good performance for smaller \alphaα\alpha values.

C.1 Data Generation




In all experiments, task parameters \theta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{d}θ∈ℝd\theta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{d} are sampled from the distribution mentioned in the main text, data sequences are sampled according to the task.
All task distributions during training are zero mean and unit variance in each dimension, except for the Volterra experiments where they are normalized to have standard deviation 0.2.
For testing, we sample \thetaθ\theta from \mathcal{N}(\mu\one,I)𝒩⁡(μ​𝟙,I)\mathcal{N}(\mu\one,I) where \mu\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}μ∈ℝ\mu\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}} is the shift value and \one𝟙\one is the all ones vector, and the data is sampled according to this task.
Unless otherwise specified, a new set of tasks \thetaθ\theta is sampled for each training iteration. Otherwise, when the number of tasks is specified, we sample that many tasks at the start of training and use those same tasks throughout training.




Linear Regression



Given a task parameter \theta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{8}θ∈ℝ8\theta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}}}^{8}, we sample x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{8})xi∼𝒩⁡(0,I8)x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{8}) and y_{i}=\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle+\epsilon_{i}yi=⟨xi,θ⟩+ϵiy_{i}=\langle x_{i},\theta\rangle+\epsilon_{i} where \epsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^{2})ϵi∼𝒩⁡(0,0.52)\epsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^{2}).
Given a context of (x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{k},y_{k})(x1,y1),…,(xk,yk)(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{k},y_{k}), the model is trained to predict y_{k+1}yk+1y_{k+1} given x_{k+1}xk+1x_{k+1} with the MSE loss.
At evaluation, we evaluate the model output against x_{i}^{\top}\thetaxi⊤​θx_{i}^{\top}\theta.
We refer to the linear regression experiments in Raventós et al. (2023) for details.
